    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.1, F31.3, F31.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello, Doctor. They... my family, I mean... they say I'm here because I used our savings on wood, lights, and all this display stuff. I was out there every night, pushing the work well after dark. I just felt like if I stopped, the whole house, maybe the whole block, would lose its chance. You know, it's funny about wood these days. Have you seen the prices at the hardware store? It's outrageous. Back when I was working... well, before I retired, you could get a good piece of plywood for next to nothing. Now? Forget about it. And the young kid working the register, he didn't even know the difference between pine and oak. I tried explaining it to him, but he just looked at me blankly. It's like nobody cares about craftsmanship anymore. Anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the house. Yeah, they weren't happy about the money.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt unusually energetic, excited, or irritable for several days or more, where it was noticeably different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yeah, energetic, exactly. Most days lately I feel completely charged up, almost joyful, really. It’s like I’m young again! Back when I was, oh, twenty-two maybe? I used to work on engines back then. Old Fords, mostly. You could actually get under the hood and fix things with a wrench, not like these modern cars that are just computers on wheels. My buddy Jimmy had this beautiful '68 Mustang... we drove it all the way down to Florida one summer. The humidity down there, though, I’ll tell ya, it just sticks right to your bones. You sweat through a shirt in five minutes. \n\nBut anyway, what was I... oh, right, the energy. Yeah, I feel fantastic, but... well, if my wife starts questioning me about what I'm doing, or if those contractors I brought in to help with the display start doubting my plans, I get hot fast. I just start yelling at them. Because they don't listen! And speaking of those contractors, the guy they sent Tuesday left his truck leaking oil all over my concrete. I told him to check his oil pan gasket, but he just ignored me. Nobody respects another man's driveway anymore, you know? Just left a big black puddle right near the rose bushes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you felt so full of energy or ideas that you didn’t need much sleep, or when you started a lot of projects at once?\n\nPatient: Sleep? I haven't needed much of that at all lately. There's just too much to do, too much going on up here! I’ve been on the phone constantly, calling all the neighbors, tracking down old photo students I used to know, hardware stores... just anyone I can get ahold of to line up exhibits and building ideas and get work crews going all at the exact same time. It’s a huge operation. \n\nYou know, speaking of old photos, I was looking through this dusty box of Polaroids the other day trying to find some reference shots. Do you remember when Polaroids were the big thing? You'd shake 'em, even though the company told you not to. My brother, Tommy, he took a picture of my dog Buster with one of those back in... oh, must have been '76. Buster was a good dog. Half beagle, half something else, nobody really knew. He used to chase the mail truck clear down to the county line every single morning. The mailman, a fella named Arthur, he actually started carrying dog biscuits just to bribe Buster. Arthur had a bad left knee, walked with a heavy limp from the war. Korea, I think it was. Or maybe it was just a bad fall off a roof? No, I'm pretty sure it was the war. Anyway, Arthur always wore those thick wool socks, even in the dead of summer, which I never understood. You'd think his feet would be roasting in those boots...\n\nDoctor: Have you been talking more than usual, or feeling like your thoughts are racing so much that you can’t keep up with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess you could say that. Once I start explaining my plans, the words just keep coming, you know? It's like I have to get it all out before the thought is gone. My wife, she gets so frustrated, she says I run right over her before she can even get a sentence in. But she doesn't understand the urgency of it all. Speaking of urgency, did I tell you about the plumbing issue we had last month? It was a disaster. The main pipe under the sink just burst right open, water everywhere. I had to call three different plumbers before someone could come out. And the guy who finally showed up, he charged me double for an emergency visit! I told him, 'Listen buddy, I could have fixed this myself if I had the right wrench.' But you know how it is, they want to squeeze every dime out of you. My dad used to be a plumber, actually. Worked for the city for thirty years. He always said the secret to a good pipe joint is the tape. You gotta wrap it tight, but not too tight, or you strip the threads. He had this old green toolbox he carried everywhere... weighed a ton. I think it's still sitting in my garage somewhere, buried under all the Christmas decorations. You ever try untangling Christmas lights? It's a nightmare. I swear they tie themselves into knots the second you put them in the box...\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you actually require very little sleep, like only a few hours a night, and still feel completely rested or energized?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I require very little sleep, no, I just... I haven't really noticed needing less, you know? I still get my rest when I can. But speaking of rest, you know what really keeps a man up at night? The crickets around here. I don't know if it's the season or what, but they are just deafening lately. You'd think they had little microphones out there in the grass. We used to catch 'em when we were kids, put 'em in Mason jars with a little grass and a twig. My sister Sarah, she hated bugs. Just terrified of anything with more than four legs. One time, I must have been about ten, I put a jar of lightning bugs right next to her bed. She screamed so loud I thought she was gonna wake the whole neighborhood. My mother gave me such a smack for that. She had this wooden spoon, right? Kept it right by the stove, specifically for when me or my brothers got out of line. It was worn down on one side from where she'd stir her Sunday gravy. Best gravy you ever tasted, too. She'd simmer it all day long with these big chunks of pork shoulder... you could smell it halfway down the block...\n\nDoctor: Have you been involved in any activities that were excessive, risky, or outside your usual behavior — like spending large amounts of money, starting many projects at once, or making big plans that others might see as unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Well, my kids, they say I’m here because I went and used up our savings on wood, lights, all this display stuff... kept pushing the work way after dark. But I felt like if I stopped, the whole house, the whole block would lose its chance! You know, speaking of the block, the guy who lives three houses down, Henderson, he’s got this old Ford truck sitting in his driveway. Hasn't moved it in five years. Just sits there rusting away, gathering leaves under the tires. I told him once, I said, 'Henderson, you ought to sell that thing for scrap or get it running.' My uncle Jimmy had a truck just like it. A '56 F-100, cherry red. He used to take us fishing up at Miller's Pond every summer. We'd pack bologna sandwiches and sit on the tailgate for hours. Jimmy was a character, always wore this greasy old baseball cap from the hardware store... the one that closed down back in '82 when the big chain moved in on the highway. It's a shame what happened to those local places. Used to be you knew the guy selling you nails, knew his kids, his dog... now it's just aisles and aisles of plastic packaging and nobody can tell you where the lag bolts are...\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least a week where your mood was clearly and consistently different—like being unusually high, overly confident, or easily annoyed—so much so that other people noticed it?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, for the last week or two, most days I feel just... charged up, you know? Almost joyful, like I’m young again, back in my twenties with all the energy in the world. But then, if my wife or those contractors question what I'm doing, I get hot real fast and just start yelling at them. They don't see the vision. Speaking of contractors, you wouldn't believe the guy they sent over to look at my roof last year. He shows up in this little compact car, barely big enough to fit a ladder, wearing these pristine white sneakers. I took one look at him and thought, 'This guy's never swung a hammer in his life.' My grandfather, now he was a real carpenter. Built his own house from the ground up, out in the country. He had hands like sandpaper and could drive a nail with two hits. He used to make us these little wooden boats out of scrap pine, and we'd sail them down the creek behind the house. That creek used to freeze solid in the winter. We'd strap on these old metal ice skates—the kind you had to use a key to tighten onto your boots—and skate until our ankles were bruised black and blue. You don't see kids doing that anymore. Now they're all inside on those little glowing screens, swiping at things. I tried to use one of those tablets once, my grandson left it on the coffee table. Couldn't figure out how to turn the darn thing off...\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time recently when your mood or behavior was so extreme that you couldn't function normally or needed to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about needing a hospital, but... I guess things have gotten a bit much at home. The house has gotten real hard for my wife and me to live in, honestly. I've got all my materials, the half-finished setups, the wiring... it's taking over the living room, the dining room, places we actually need to use. And people I care about, my kids, the neighbors, they're avoiding me now after all the arguments we've had about it. Which reminds me, my neighbor's kid, Tommy, he used to come over all the time to help me rake leaves. Good kid, always wore this bright yellow raincoat when it drizzled. His mother bought it for him at that department store downtown, the one with the revolving doors. I always hated those doors. You ever get stuck in one with someone moving too slow? Happened to me back in '95 with this fella carrying a giant tuba case. A tuba! Who carries a tuba through a revolving door? He said he was playing in a brass band for some parade. Parades used to be so grand, with the floats made of real tissue paper flowers. My aunt Clara used to make those flowers by the hundreds. She had arthritis so bad her knuckles looked like walnuts, but she'd sit there twisting paper all afternoon, watching her soap operas. Those stories never made any sense to me, people coming back from the dead, evil twins... but Clara, she'd cry over 'em like they were family...\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself more restless or unable to sit still lately, or been moving around more than usual, like pacing or fidgeting?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, sitting still just isn't in the cards for me right now. I can’t just sit in the chair anymore, you know? I’m up and moving all day long—from the garden to the darkroom to the porch, carrying tools, fixing things before anyone even asks me to. Just go, go, go. Speaking of tools, I found this old rusted wrench in the dirt the other day. Reminded me of the one my dad used to keep in his glovebox. He drove this heavy old Buick, a real land yacht, painted a color he called 'seafoam.' Man, that car could float over potholes like they weren't even there. We took it all the way to Mount Rushmore back in the summer of '68. The radiator boiled over twice in South Dakota. Dad had to use our drinking water to cool it down, so we were all parched by the time we got to the motel. That motel had this glowing neon sign, a big cowboy swinging a lasso, but half the bulbs were burnt out so it just looked like he was waving a noodle. You don't see signs like that anymore. Now it's all those bright LED billboards that blind you when you're driving at night. I almost ran off the shoulder last week looking at one advertising a lawyer. Who needs a billboard that bright for a lawyer? If you need a lawyer, you look in the phone book. At least, that's what we used to do. Now they don't even deliver phone books...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a specific time in the last couple of weeks when your energy and mood were so high that it was completely different from your usual self—and did that last for a full week or more without breaking?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it hasn't just been a week, doc. This whole... feeling, this energy, it's been going on for about three months now. Ever since the remodeling started and everything in the house got turned completely upside down. You know how it is when you start a project, you pull up one floorboard and suddenly you're rewiring the whole downstairs. Speaking of wires, I found this old spool of copper wire in the basement. Thick stuff, the kind they used to use back when things were built to last. My uncle Arthur, he was an electrician down at the old manufacturing plant. He used to bring home scraps of copper and weave them into these intricate little baskets. He had hands like a magician, Arthur did. He could fix a toaster with a paperclip and a piece of chewing gum. He always chewed that Black Jack gum, the licorice kind. Smelled awful, if you ask me, but he swore by it. Said it kept his teeth strong. Course, he ended up getting dentures anyway by the time he was sixty. Those dentures used to whistle when he said his 'S's. We kids used to try and mimic him, whistling through our teeth while we played out in the dirt. Dirt was different back then, I swear. Richer, darker. We'd dig these massive holes in the vacant lot next door, trying to reach China. Never did make it, obviously. But we found an old glass medicine bottle once. Blue glass, real pretty. I kept it on my windowsill for years until the cat knocked it over...\n\nDoctor: During this time when you've felt so energized and full of ideas, have you found that you actually needed less sleep—like only a couple of hours a night—and still felt completely rested or even hyper?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really noticed needing less sleep, to be honest. I mean, I sleep when I sleep, you know? Sometimes I'm just lying there thinking about the roof. We had this terrible storm a few years back that took off half the shingles on the south side. The roofer who came out to fix it, he drove this beat-up old pickup truck with a bumper sticker that said 'I'd rather be fishing.' I always wondered what kind of fish he liked to catch. My grandfather used to take me trout fishing up at the lake when I was a boy. We'd get up before the sun, when the air was still cold and smelled like pine needles. He had this old tackle box that clicked when you opened it, full of these shiny lures with feathers tied to them. He called them 'flies,' but they didn't look like any fly I ever saw. Real colorful things, reds and yellows. Yellow always reminds me of my wife's old apron, the one she used to wear when she baked those peach cobblers on Sundays. The smell of cinnamon would just fill up the whole house, right out to the front porch where I'd be sitting on the swing. That swing squeaked on the left side, no matter how much oil I put on the chain...\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you actually require very little sleep, like only a few hours a night, and still feel completely rested or even energized?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, I haven't really noticed needing less sleep. I mean, I sleep my usual amount, I guess. It's just hard to say exactly how many hours because I don't really look at the clock. We used to have this big grandfather clock in the hallway, right by the front door. Chipped mahogany wood. My wife bought it at an estate sale from this old lady who claimed it came over on a boat from England. I don't know about all that, but it chimed every hour on the hour. Loud, too. You could hear it all the way upstairs in the guest bedroom. We had to put a folded-up matchbook under one of the feet to keep it from wobbling when someone walked past. Those matchbooks you used to get at restaurants, the ones with the name printed on the front. We had a whole bowl of them by the telephone. Back when phones had cords and you had to stretch them across the kitchen just to look out the window while you talked...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time in the past few months when your mood was consistently much higher than usual—like being unusually excited or full of ideas—and you were doing a lot more than you normally would, almost nonstop, for days or weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, most days I feel completely charged up, you know? Almost joyful, really. It’s like I’m young again, back when I could work a full shift and still have energy to spare. But I’ll tell you what, if my wife or those contractors question what I'm doing with the remodeling, I get hot fast. I just start yelling. I don't mean to, but it just bubbles up. Speaking of bubbles, we used to have this old clawfoot tub in the bathroom upstairs. Cast iron, weighed a ton. Took four men to get it up the stairs when we moved in. One of them, a big fella named Earl, he slipped on the landing and put a hole right through the drywall with his elbow. We never did fix that hole properly. Just hung a picture of a sailboat over it. My wife loved sailboats. She always wanted to go to Maine and see the schooners. We had a brochure for Maine sitting on the coffee table for years. It had a picture of a lighthouse on the front, one of those tall white ones with the red stripe. I always wondered how many stairs were inside a lighthouse. Must be hundreds. Imagine having to carry groceries up all those stairs...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a specific time in the last few months when your mood was so high and you were doing so much that it was clearly different from your usual self—and did that last for a full week or more without breaking?\n\nPatient: Well, a week? Heck, it’s been way longer than that. This has been going on for about three months now, ever since the remodeling started and everything in the house got turned upside down. I mean, literally upside down—they had my good La-Z-Boy flipped right over on its head in the dining room just to lay the new floorboards. That chair is olive green, bought it at Sears back in '98. You remember when Sears used to have those big thick catalogs? You could order just about anything out of those pages. Tools, clothes, even whole houses back in the day. My mother used to let me cut pictures out of the old catalogs before she put them in the woodstove to burn. I’d cut out the bicycles, mostly. I had this bright red Schwinn when I was a boy. It had one of those silver bells on the handlebars that rang real sharp, like a fire engine. We used to ride out and chase the fire engines down Elm Street whenever we heard the sirens going. The fire chief was a fella named Sullivan, had a big bushy mustache that covered his whole mouth. Looked just like a walrus, I swear. He used to sit outside the station on this folding metal chair, the kind they have at church bingo nights...\n\nDoctor: Have you actually needed less sleep during this time—like only a couple of hours a night—and still felt completely rested or even energized?\n\nPatient: I thought we already talked about the sleeping... I really haven't noticed needing less sleep or anything like that. I just lay down when I lay down. Course, my bed ain't exactly a luxury hotel, it's one of those old spring mattresses. You know the kind where you can feel every single coil poking right into your ribs? Reminds me of the bench seat in my buddy Ray's old Chevy pickup. Man, that truck had the worst suspension you ever saw. Every time you hit a pothole, your teeth would rattle. We used to drive it down to the county dump on Saturday mornings to throw out scrap wood and old pipes. The dump always smelled like burning tires and wet cardboard. And there were seagulls everywhere. Hundreds of 'em, just screeching and diving at the garbage piles. I never understood why seagulls hang around a landfill when the ocean is only fifty miles away. Seems like they'd rather eat stale hotdog buns than fresh fish. We used to go fishing down at Miller's Pond when I was a teenager. Caught a catfish once that was as long as my arm. Had these big old whiskers on it, looked just like a river monster. We had to use this thick nylon line just to reel him in, otherwise he would have snapped it right in half...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Couldn't figure out how to turn the darn thing off...\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time recently when your mood or behavior was so extreme that you couldn't function normally or needed to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about needing a hospital, but... I guess things have gotten a bit much at home. The house has gotten real hard for my wife and me to live in, honestly. I've got all my materials, the half-finished setups, the wiring... it's taking over the living room, the dining room, places we actually need to use. And people I care about, my kids, the neighbors, they're avoiding me now after all the arguments we've had about it. Which reminds me, my neighbor's kid, Tommy, he used to come over all the time to help me rake leaves. Good kid, always wore this bright yellow raincoat when it drizzled. His mother bought it for him at that department store downtown, the one with the revolving doors. I always hated those doors. You ever get stuck in one with someone moving too slow? Happened to me back in '95 with this fella carrying a giant tuba case. A tuba! Who carries a tuba through a revolving door? He said he was playing in a brass band for some parade. Parades used to be so grand, with the floats made of real tissue paper flowers. My aunt Clara used to make those flowers by the hundreds. She had arthritis so bad her knuckles looked like walnuts, but she'd sit there twisting paper all afternoon, watching her soap operas. Those stories never made any sense to me, people coming back from the dead, evil twins... but Clara, she'd cry over 'em like they were family...\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself more restless or unable to sit still lately, or been moving around more than usual, like pacing or fidgeting?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, sitting still just isn't in the cards for me right now. I can’t just sit in the chair anymore, you know? I’m up and moving all day long—from the garden to the darkroom to the porch, carrying tools, fixing things before anyone even asks me to. Just go, go, go. Speaking of tools, I found this old rusted wrench in the dirt the other day. Reminded me of the one my dad used to keep in his glovebox. He drove this heavy old Buick, a real land yacht, painted a color he called 'seafoam.' Man, that car could float over potholes like they weren't even there. We took it all the way to Mount Rushmore back in the summer of '68. The radiator boiled over twice in South Dakota. Dad had to use our drinking water to cool it down, so we were all parched by the time we got to the motel. That motel had this glowing neon sign, a big cowboy swinging a lasso, but half the bulbs were burnt out so it just looked like he was waving a noodle. You don't see signs like that anymore. Now it's all those bright LED billboards that blind you when you're driving at night. I almost ran off the shoulder last week looking at one advertising a lawyer. Who needs a billboard that bright for a lawyer? If you need a lawyer, you look in the phone book. At least, that's what we used to do. Now they don't even deliver phone books...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a specific time in the last couple of weeks when your energy and mood were so high that it was completely different from your usual self—and did that last for a full week or more without breaking?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it hasn't just been a week, doc. This whole... feeling, this energy, it's been going on for about three months now. Ever since the remodeling started and everything in the house got turned completely upside down. You know how it is when you start a project, you pull up one floorboard and suddenly you're rewiring the whole downstairs. Speaking of wires, I found this old spool of copper wire in the basement. Thick stuff, the kind they used to use back when things were built to last. My uncle Arthur, he was an electrician down at the old manufacturing plant. He used to bring home scraps of copper and weave them into these intricate little baskets. He had hands like a magician, Arthur did. He could fix a toaster with a paperclip and a piece of chewing gum. He always chewed that Black Jack gum, the licorice kind. Smelled awful, if you ask me, but he swore by it. Said it kept his teeth strong. Course, he ended up getting dentures anyway by the time he was sixty. Those dentures used to whistle when he said his 'S's. We kids used to try and mimic him, whistling through our teeth while we played out in the dirt. Dirt was different back then, I swear. Richer, darker. We'd dig these massive holes in the vacant lot next door, trying to reach China. Never did make it, obviously. But we found an old glass medicine bottle once. Blue glass, real pretty. I kept it on my windowsill for years until the cat knocked it over...\n\nDoctor: During this time when you've felt so energized and full of ideas, have you found that you actually needed less sleep—like only a couple of hours a night—and still felt completely rested or even hyper?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really noticed needing less sleep, to be honest. I mean, I sleep when I sleep, you know? Sometimes I'm just lying there thinking about the roof. We had this terrible storm a few years back that took off half the shingles on the south side. The roofer who came out to fix it, he drove this beat-up old pickup truck with a bumper sticker that said 'I'd rather be fishing.' I always wondered what kind of fish he liked to catch. My grandfather used to take me trout fishing up at the lake when I was a boy. We'd get up before the sun, when the air was still cold and smelled like pine needles. He had this old tackle box that clicked when you opened it, full of these shiny lures with feathers tied to them. He called them 'flies,' but they didn't look like any fly I ever saw. Real colorful things, reds and yellows. Yellow always reminds me of my wife's old apron, the one she used to wear when she baked those peach cobblers on Sundays. The smell of cinnamon would just fill up the whole house, right out to the front porch where I'd be sitting on the swing. That swing squeaked on the left side, no matter how much oil I put on the chain...\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you actually require very little sleep, like only a few hours a night, and still feel completely rested or even energized?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, I haven't really noticed needing less sleep. I mean, I sleep my usual amount, I guess. It's just hard to say exactly how many hours because I don't really look at the clock. We used to have this big grandfather clock in the hallway, right by the front door. Chipped mahogany wood. My wife bought it at an estate sale from this old lady who claimed it came over on a boat from England. I don't know about all that, but it chimed every hour on the hour. Loud, too. You could hear it all the way upstairs in the guest bedroom. We had to put a folded-up matchbook under one of the feet to keep it from wobbling when someone walked past. Those matchbooks you used to get at restaurants, the ones with the name printed on the front. We had a whole bowl of them by the telephone. Back when phones had cords and you had to stretch them across the kitchen just to look out the window while you talked...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time in the past few months when your mood was consistently much higher than usual—like being unusually excited or full of ideas—and you were doing a lot more than you normally would, almost nonstop, for days or weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, most days I feel completely charged up, you know? Almost joyful, really. It’s like I’m young again, back when I could work a full shift and still have energy to spare. But I’ll tell you what, if my wife or those contractors question what I'm doing with the remodeling, I get hot fast. I just start yelling. I don't mean to, but it just bubbles up. Speaking of bubbles, we used to have this old clawfoot tub in the bathroom upstairs. Cast iron, weighed a ton. Took four men to get it up the stairs when we moved in. One of them, a big fella named Earl, he slipped on the landing and put a hole right through the drywall with his elbow. We never did fix that hole properly. Just hung a picture of a sailboat over it. My wife loved sailboats. She always wanted to go to Maine and see the schooners. We had a brochure for Maine sitting on the coffee table for years. It had a picture of a lighthouse on the front, one of those tall white ones with the red stripe. I always wondered how many stairs were inside a lighthouse. Must be hundreds. Imagine having to carry groceries up all those stairs...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a specific time in the last few months when your mood was so high and you were doing so much that it was clearly different from your usual self—and did that last for a full week or more without breaking?\n\nPatient: Well, a week? Heck, it’s been way longer than that. This has been going on for about three months now, ever since the remodeling started and everything in the house got turned upside down. I mean, literally upside down—they had my good La-Z-Boy flipped right over on its head in the dining room just to lay the new floorboards. That chair is olive green, bought it at Sears back in '98. You remember when Sears used to have those big thick catalogs? You could order just about anything out of those pages. Tools, clothes, even whole houses back in the day. My mother used to let me cut pictures out of the old catalogs before she put them in the woodstove to burn. I’d cut out the bicycles, mostly. I had this bright red Schwinn when I was a boy. It had one of those silver bells on the handlebars that rang real sharp, like a fire engine. We used to ride out and chase the fire engines down Elm Street whenever we heard the sirens going. The fire chief was a fella named Sullivan, had a big bushy mustache that covered his whole mouth. Looked just like a walrus, I swear. He used to sit outside the station on this folding metal chair, the kind they have at church bingo nights...\n\nDoctor: Have you actually needed less sleep during this time—like only a couple of hours a night—and still felt completely rested or even energized?\n\nPatient: I thought we already talked about the sleeping... I really haven't noticed needing less sleep or anything like that. I just lay down when I lay down. Course, my bed ain't exactly a luxury hotel, it's one of those old spring mattresses. You know the kind where you can feel every single coil poking right into your ribs? Reminds me of the bench seat in my buddy Ray's old Chevy pickup. Man, that truck had the worst suspension you ever saw. Every time you hit a pothole, your teeth would rattle. We used to drive it down to the county dump on Saturday mornings to throw out scrap wood and old pipes. The dump always smelled like burning tires and wet cardboard. And there were seagulls everywhere. Hundreds of 'em, just screeching and diving at the garbage piles. I never understood why seagulls hang around a landfill when the ocean is only fifty miles away. Seems like they'd rather eat stale hotdog buns than fresh fish. We used to go fishing down at Miller's Pond when I was a teenager. Caught a catfish once that was as long as my arm. Had these big old whiskers on it, looked just like a river monster. We had to use this thick nylon line just to reel him in, otherwise he would have snapped it right in half...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, yeah, it's like I was saying, it's been months of this. I just can't sit still anymore. I used to be able to just sit in my chair, but now I'm up moving constantly. I'll go from the garden, out to the darkroom, then over to the porch. I'm always carrying tools around, fixing things before anyone even asks me to. Speaking of tools, I found this old rusted wrench in the dirt out by the tomato plants the other day. Heavy thing, probably belonged to the guy who owned the house before us. He was a mechanic, I think. Had a whole garage full of car parts. My dad was a mechanic, too, worked down at the Ford dealership for thirty years. He used to come home smelling like motor oil and cheap coffee. We had this big oak tree in the front yard, and he tied a tire swing to one of the branches. Not a regular tire, mind you, it was a tractor tire. Huge black thing. You could fit three kids inside it if you squeezed tight enough. Course, after it rained, the inside would fill up with water and mosquitoes. Nasty little buggers, mosquitoes. They always seemed to bite me more than anyone else. My mother used to rub vinegar on the bites, said it stopped the itching. Smelled awful, though, like a pickle jar. I never could eat pickles after that...

They... my family, I mean... they say I'm here because I used our savings on wood, lights, and all this display stuff. I was out there every night, pushing the work well after dark. I just felt like if I stopped, the whole house, maybe the whole block, would lose its chance. You know, it's funny about wood these days. Have you seen the prices at the hardware store? It's outrageous. Back when I was working... well, before I retired, you could get a good piece of plywood for next to nothing. Now? Forget about it. And the young kid working the register, he didn't even know the difference between pine and oak. I tried explaining it to him, but he just looked at me blankly. It's like nobody cares about craftsmanship anymore. Anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the house. Yeah, they weren't happy about the money.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt unusually energetic, excited, or irritable for several days or more, where it was noticeably different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yeah, energetic, exactly. Most days lately I feel completely charged up, almost joyful, really. It’s like I’m young again! Back when I was, oh, twenty-two maybe? I used to work on engines back then. Old Fords, mostly. You could actually get under the hood and fix things with a wrench, not like these modern cars that are just computers on wheels. My buddy Jimmy had this beautiful '68 Mustang... we drove it all the way down to Florida one summer. The humidity down there, though, I’ll tell ya, it just sticks right to your bones. You sweat through a shirt in five minutes. \n\nBut anyway, what was I... oh, right, the energy. Yeah, I feel fantastic, but... well, if my wife starts questioning me about what I'm doing, or if those contractors I brought in to help with the display start doubting my plans, I get hot fast. I just start yelling at them. Because they don't listen! And speaking of those contractors, the guy they sent Tuesday left his truck leaking oil all over my concrete. I told him to check his oil pan gasket, but he just ignored me. Nobody respects another man's driveway anymore, you know? Just left a big black puddle right near the rose bushes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you felt so full of energy or ideas that you didn’t need much sleep, or when you started a lot of projects at once?\n\nPatient: Sleep? I haven't needed much of that at all lately. There's just too much to do, too much going on up here! I’ve been on the phone constantly, calling all the neighbors, tracking down old photo students I used to know, hardware stores... just anyone I can get ahold of to line up exhibits and building ideas and get work crews going all at the exact same time. It’s a huge operation. \n\nYou know, speaking of old photos, I was looking through this dusty box of Polaroids the other day trying to find some reference shots. Do you remember when Polaroids were the big thing? You'd shake 'em, even though the company told you not to. My brother, Tommy, he took a picture of my dog Buster with one of those back in... oh, must have been '76. Buster was a good dog. Half beagle, half something else, nobody really knew. He used to chase the mail truck clear down to the county line every single morning. The mailman, a fella named Arthur, he actually started carrying dog biscuits just to bribe Buster. Arthur had a bad left knee, walked with a heavy limp from the war. Korea, I think it was. Or maybe it was just a bad fall off a roof? No, I'm pretty sure it was the war. Anyway, Arthur always wore those thick wool socks, even in the dead of summer, which I never understood. You'd think his feet would be roasting in those boots...\n\nDoctor: Have you been talking more than usual, or feeling like your thoughts are racing so much that you can’t keep up with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess you could say that. Once I start explaining my plans, the words just keep coming, you know? It's like I have to get it all out before the thought is gone. My wife, she gets so frustrated, she says I run right over her before she can even get a sentence in. But she doesn't understand the urgency of it all. Speaking of urgency, did I tell you about the plumbing issue we had last month? It was a disaster. The main pipe under the sink just burst right open, water everywhere. I had to call three different plumbers before someone could come out. And the guy who finally showed up, he charged me double for an emergency visit! I told him, 'Listen buddy, I could have fixed this myself if I had the right wrench.' But you know how it is, they want to squeeze every dime out of you. My dad used to be a plumber, actually. Worked for the city for thirty years. He always said the secret to a good pipe joint is the tape. You gotta wrap it tight, but not too tight, or you strip the threads. He had this old green toolbox he carried everywhere... weighed a ton. I think it's still sitting in my garage somewhere, buried under all the Christmas decorations. You ever try untangling Christmas lights? It's a nightmare. I swear they tie themselves into knots the second you put them in the box...\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you actually require very little sleep, like only a few hours a night, and still feel completely rested or energized?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I require very little sleep, no, I just... I haven't really noticed needing less, you know? I still get my rest when I can. But speaking of rest, you know what really keeps a man up at night? The crickets around here. I don't know if it's the season or what, but they are just deafening lately. You'd think they had little microphones out there in the grass. We used to catch 'em when we were kids, put 'em in Mason jars with a little grass and a twig. My sister Sarah, she hated bugs. Just terrified of anything with more than four legs. One time, I must have been about ten, I put a jar of lightning bugs right next to her bed. She screamed so loud I thought she was gonna wake the whole neighborhood. My mother gave me such a smack for that. She had this wooden spoon, right? Kept it right by the stove, specifically for when me or my brothers got out of line. It was worn down on one side from where she'd stir her Sunday gravy. Best gravy you ever tasted, too. She'd simmer it all day long with these big chunks of pork shoulder... you could smell it halfway down the block...\n\nDoctor: Have you been involved in any activities that were excessive, risky, or outside your usual behavior — like spending large amounts of money, starting many projects at once, or making big plans that others might see as unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Well, my kids, they say I’m here because I went and used up our savings on wood, lights, all this display stuff... kept pushing the work way after dark. But I felt like if I stopped, the whole house, the whole block would lose its chance! You know, speaking of the block, the guy who lives three houses down, Henderson, he’s got this old Ford truck sitting in his driveway. Hasn't moved it in five years. Just sits there rusting away, gathering leaves under the tires. I told him once, I said, 'Henderson, you ought to sell that thing for scrap or get it running.' My uncle Jimmy had a truck just like it. A '56 F-100, cherry red. He used to take us fishing up at Miller's Pond every summer. We'd pack bologna sandwiches and sit on the tailgate for hours. Jimmy was a character, always wore this greasy old baseball cap from the hardware store... the one that closed down back in '82 when the big chain moved in on the highway. It's a shame what happened to those local places. Used to be you knew the guy selling you nails, knew his kids, his dog... now it's just aisles and aisles of plastic packaging and nobody can tell you where the lag bolts are...\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least a week where your mood was clearly and consistently different—like being unusually high, overly confident, or easily annoyed—so much so that other people noticed it?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, for the last week or two, most days I feel just... charged up, you know? Almost joyful, like I’m young again, back in my twenties with all the energy in the world. But then, if my wife or those contractors question what I'm doing, I get hot real fast and just start yelling at them. They don't see the vision. Speaking of contractors, you wouldn't believe the guy they sent over to look at my roof last year. He shows up in this little compact car, barely big enough to fit a ladder, wearing these pristine white sneakers. I took one look at him and thought, 'This guy's never swung a hammer in his life.' My grandfather, now he was a real carpenter. Built his own house from the ground up, out in the country. He had hands like sandpaper and could drive a nail with two hits. He used to make us these little wooden boats out of scrap pine, and we'd sail them down the creek behind the house. That creek used to freeze solid in the winter. We'd strap on these old metal ice skates—the kind you had to use a key to tighten onto your boots—and skate until our ankles were bruised black and blue. You don't see kids doing that anymore. Now they're all inside on those little glowing screens, swiping at things. I tried to use one of those tablets once, my grandson left it on the coffee table. Couldn't figure out how to turn the darn thing off...\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time recently when your mood or behavior was so extreme that you couldn't function normally or needed to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about needing a hospital, but... I guess things have gotten a bit much at home. The house has gotten real hard for my wife and me to live in, honestly. I've got all my materials, the half-finished setups, the wiring... it's taking over the living room, the dining room, places we actually need to use. And people I care about, my kids, the neighbors, they're avoiding me now after all the arguments we've had about it. Which reminds me, my neighbor's kid, Tommy, he used to come over all the time to help me rake leaves. Good kid, always wore this bright yellow raincoat when it drizzled. His mother bought it for him at that department store downtown, the one with the revolving doors. I always hated those doors. You ever get stuck in one with someone moving too slow? Happened to me back in '95 with this fella carrying a giant tuba case. A tuba! Who carries a tuba through a revolving door? He said he was playing in a brass band for some parade. Parades used to be so grand, with the floats made of real tissue paper flowers. My aunt Clara used to make those flowers by the hundreds. She had arthritis so bad her knuckles looked like walnuts, but she'd sit there twisting paper all afternoon, watching her soap operas. Those stories never made any sense to me, people coming back from the dead, evil twins... but Clara, she'd cry over 'em like they were family...\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself more restless or unable to sit still lately, or been moving around more than usual, like pacing or fidgeting?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, sitting still just isn't in the cards for me right now. I can’t just sit in the chair anymore, you know? I’m up and moving all day long—from the garden to the darkroom to the porch, carrying tools, fixing things before anyone even asks me to. Just go, go, go. Speaking of tools, I found this old rusted wrench in the dirt the other day. Reminded me of the one my dad used to keep in his glovebox. He drove this heavy old Buick, a real land yacht, painted a color he called 'seafoam.' Man, that car could float over potholes like they weren't even there. We took it all the way to Mount Rushmore back in the summer of '68. The radiator boiled over twice in South Dakota. Dad had to use our drinking water to cool it down, so we were all parched by the time we got to the motel. That motel had this glowing neon sign, a big cowboy swinging a lasso, but half the bulbs were burnt out so it just looked like he was waving a noodle. You don't see signs like that anymore. Now it's all those bright LED billboards that blind you when you're driving at night. I almost ran off the shoulder last week looking at one advertising a lawyer. Who needs a billboard that bright for a lawyer? If you need a lawyer, you look in the phone book. At least, that's what we used to do. Now they don't even deliver phone books...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a specific time in the last couple of weeks when your energy and mood were so high that it was completely different from your usual self—and did that last for a full week or more without breaking?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it hasn't just been a week, doc. This whole... feeling, this energy, it's been going on for about three months now. Ever since the remodeling started and everything in the house got turned completely upside down. You know how it is when you start a project, you pull up one floorboard and suddenly you're rewiring the whole downstairs. Speaking of wires, I found this old spool of copper wire in the basement. Thick stuff, the kind they used to use back when things were built to last. My uncle Arthur, he was an electrician down at the old manufacturing plant. He used to bring home scraps of copper and weave them into these intricate little baskets. He had hands like a magician, Arthur did. He could fix a toaster with a paperclip and a piece of chewing gum. He always chewed that Black Jack gum, the licorice kind. Smelled awful, if you ask me, but he swore by it. Said it kept his teeth strong. Course, he ended up getting dentures anyway by the time he was sixty. Those dentures used to whistle when he said his 'S's. We kids used to try and mimic him, whistling through our teeth while we played out in the dirt. Dirt was different back then, I swear. Richer, darker. We'd dig these massive holes in the vacant lot next door, trying to reach China. Never did make it, obviously. But we found an old glass medicine bottle once. Blue glass, real pretty. I kept it on my windowsill for years until the cat knocked it over...\n\nDoctor: During this time when you've felt so energized and full of ideas, have you found that you actually needed less sleep—like only a couple of hours a night—and still felt completely rested or even hyper?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really noticed needing less sleep, to be honest. I mean, I sleep when I sleep, you know? Sometimes I'm just lying there thinking about the roof. We had this terrible storm a few years back that took off half the shingles on the south side. The roofer who came out to fix it, he drove this beat-up old pickup truck with a bumper sticker that said 'I'd rather be fishing.' I always wondered what kind of fish he liked to catch. My grandfather used to take me trout fishing up at the lake when I was a boy. We'd get up before the sun, when the air was still cold and smelled like pine needles. He had this old tackle box that clicked when you opened it, full of these shiny lures with feathers tied to them. He called them 'flies,' but they didn't look like any fly I ever saw. Real colorful things, reds and yellows. Yellow always reminds me of my wife's old apron, the one she used to wear when she baked those peach cobblers on Sundays. The smell of cinnamon would just fill up the whole house, right out to the front porch where I'd be sitting on the swing. That swing squeaked on the left side, no matter how much oil I put on the chain...\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you actually require very little sleep, like only a few hours a night, and still feel completely rested or even energized?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, I haven't really noticed needing less sleep. I mean, I sleep my usual amount, I guess. It's just hard to say exactly how many hours because I don't really look at the clock. We used to have this big grandfather clock in the hallway, right by the front door. Chipped mahogany wood. My wife bought it at an estate sale from this old lady who claimed it came over on a boat from England. I don't know about all that, but it chimed every hour on the hour. Loud, too. You could hear it all the way upstairs in the guest bedroom. We had to put a folded-up matchbook under one of the feet to keep it from wobbling when someone walked past. Those matchbooks you used to get at restaurants, the ones with the name printed on the front. We had a whole bowl of them by the telephone. Back when phones had cords and you had to stretch them across the kitchen just to look out the window while you talked...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time in the past few months when your mood was consistently much higher than usual—like being unusually excited or full of ideas—and you were doing a lot more than you normally would, almost nonstop, for days or weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, most days I feel completely charged up, you know? Almost joyful, really. It’s like I’m young again, back when I could work a full shift and still have energy to spare. But I’ll tell you what, if my wife or those contractors question what I'm doing with the remodeling, I get hot fast. I just start yelling. I don't mean to, but it just bubbles up. Speaking of bubbles, we used to have this old clawfoot tub in the bathroom upstairs. Cast iron, weighed a ton. Took four men to get it up the stairs when we moved in. One of them, a big fella named Earl, he slipped on the landing and put a hole right through the drywall with his elbow. We never did fix that hole properly. Just hung a picture of a sailboat over it. My wife loved sailboats. She always wanted to go to Maine and see the schooners. We had a brochure for Maine sitting on the coffee table for years. It had a picture of a lighthouse on the front, one of those tall white ones with the red stripe. I always wondered how many stairs were inside a lighthouse. Must be hundreds. Imagine having to carry groceries up all those stairs...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a specific time in the last few months when your mood was so high and you were doing so much that it was clearly different from your usual self—and did that last for a full week or more without breaking?\n\nPatient: Well, a week? Heck, it’s been way longer than that. This has been going on for about three months now, ever since the remodeling started and everything in the house got turned upside down. I mean, literally upside down—they had my good La-Z-Boy flipped right over on its head in the dining room just to lay the new floorboards. That chair is olive green, bought it at Sears back in '98. You remember when Sears used to have those big thick catalogs? You could order just about anything out of those pages. Tools, clothes, even whole houses back in the day. My mother used to let me cut pictures out of the old catalogs before she put them in the woodstove to burn. I’d cut out the bicycles, mostly. I had this bright red Schwinn when I was a boy. It had one of those silver bells on the handlebars that rang real sharp, like a fire engine. We used to ride out and chase the fire engines down Elm Street whenever we heard the sirens going. The fire chief was a fella named Sullivan, had a big bushy mustache that covered his whole mouth. Looked just like a walrus, I swear. He used to sit outside the station on this folding metal chair, the kind they have at church bingo nights...\n\nDoctor: Have you actually needed less sleep during this time—like only a couple of hours a night—and still felt completely rested or even energized?\n\nPatient: I thought we already talked about the sleeping... I really haven't noticed needing less sleep or anything like that. I just lay down when I lay down. Course, my bed ain't exactly a luxury hotel, it's one of those old spring mattresses. You know the kind where you can feel every single coil poking right into your ribs? Reminds me of the bench seat in my buddy Ray's old Chevy pickup. Man, that truck had the worst suspension you ever saw. Every time you hit a pothole, your teeth would rattle. We used to drive it down to the county dump on Saturday mornings to throw out scrap wood and old pipes. The dump always smelled like burning tires and wet cardboard. And there were seagulls everywhere. Hundreds of 'em, just screeching and diving at the garbage piles. I never understood why seagulls hang around a landfill when the ocean is only fifty miles away. Seems like they'd rather eat stale hotdog buns than fresh fish. We used to go fishing down at Miller's Pond when I was a teenager. Caught a catfish once that was as long as my arm. Had these big old whiskers on it, looked just like a river monster. We had to use this thick nylon line just to reel him in, otherwise he would have snapped it right in half...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a specific time in the past few months when your mood was clearly and consistently different—like being unusually high, overly confident, or easily annoyed—for at least a full week, and you were doing a lot more than you normally would, almost nonstop?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it's like I was saying, it's been months of this. I just can't sit still anymore. I used to be able to just sit in my chair, but now I'm up moving constantly. I'll go from the garden, out to the darkroom, then over to the porch. I'm always carrying tools around, fixing things before anyone even asks me to. Speaking of tools, I found this old rusted wrench in the dirt out by the tomato plants the other day. Heavy thing, probably belonged to the guy who owned the house before us. He was a mechanic, I think. Had a whole garage full of car parts. My dad was a mechanic, too, worked down at the Ford dealership for thirty years. He used to come home smelling like motor oil and cheap coffee. We had this big oak tree in the front yard, and he tied a tire swing to one of the branches. Not a regular tire, mind you, it was a tractor tire. Huge black thing. You could fit three kids inside it if you squeezed tight enough. Course, after it rained, the inside would fill up with water and mosquitoes. Nasty little buggers, mosquitoes. They always seemed to bite me more than anyone else. My mother used to rub vinegar on the bites, said it stopped the itching. Smelled awful, though, like a pickle jar. I never could eat pickles after that...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. He shows up in this little compact car, barely big enough to fit a ladder, wearing these pristine white sneakers. I took one look at him and thought, 'This guy's never swung a hammer in his life.' My grandfather, now he was a real carpenter. Built his own house from the ground up, out in the country. He had hands like sandpaper and could drive a nail with two hits. He used to make us these little wooden boats out of scrap pine, and we'd sail them down the creek behind the house. That creek used to freeze solid in the winter. We'd strap on these old metal ice skates—the kind you had to use a key to tighten onto your boots—and skate until our ankles were bruised black and blue. You don't see kids doing that anymore. Now they're all inside on those little glowing screens, swiping at things. I tried to use one of those tablets once, my grandson left it on the coffee table. Couldn't figure out how to turn the darn thing off...\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time recently when your mood or behavior was so extreme that you couldn't function normally or needed to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about needing a hospital, but... I guess things have gotten a bit much at home. The house has gotten real hard for my wife and me to live in, honestly. I've got all my materials, the half-finished setups, the wiring... it's taking over the living room, the dining room, places we actually need to use. And people I care about, my kids, the neighbors, they're avoiding me now after all the arguments we've had about it. Which reminds me, my neighbor's kid, Tommy, he used to come over all the time to help me rake leaves. Good kid, always wore this bright yellow raincoat when it drizzled. His mother bought it for him at that department store downtown, the one with the revolving doors. I always hated those doors. You ever get stuck in one with someone moving too slow? Happened to me back in '95 with this fella carrying a giant tuba case. A tuba! Who carries a tuba through a revolving door? He said he was playing in a brass band for some parade. Parades used to be so grand, with the floats made of real tissue paper flowers. My aunt Clara used to make those flowers by the hundreds. She had arthritis so bad her knuckles looked like walnuts, but she'd sit there twisting paper all afternoon, watching her soap operas. Those stories never made any sense to me, people coming back from the dead, evil twins... but Clara, she'd cry over 'em like they were family...\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself more restless or unable to sit still lately, or been moving around more than usual, like pacing or fidgeting?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, sitting still just isn't in the cards for me right now. I can’t just sit in the chair anymore, you know? I’m up and moving all day long—from the garden to the darkroom to the porch, carrying tools, fixing things before anyone even asks me to. Just go, go, go. Speaking of tools, I found this old rusted wrench in the dirt the other day. Reminded me of the one my dad used to keep in his glovebox. He drove this heavy old Buick, a real land yacht, painted a color he called 'seafoam.' Man, that car could float over potholes like they weren't even there. We took it all the way to Mount Rushmore back in the summer of '68. The radiator boiled over twice in South Dakota. Dad had to use our drinking water to cool it down, so we were all parched by the time we got to the motel. That motel had this glowing neon sign, a big cowboy swinging a lasso, but half the bulbs were burnt out so it just looked like he was waving a noodle. You don't see signs like that anymore. Now it's all those bright LED billboards that blind you when you're driving at night. I almost ran off the shoulder last week looking at one advertising a lawyer. Who needs a billboard that bright for a lawyer? If you need a lawyer, you look in the phone book. At least, that's what we used to do. Now they don't even deliver phone books...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a specific time in the last couple of weeks when your energy and mood were so high that it was completely different from your usual self—and did that last for a full week or more without breaking?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it hasn't just been a week, doc. This whole... feeling, this energy, it's been going on for about three months now. Ever since the remodeling started and everything in the house got turned completely upside down. You know how it is when you start a project, you pull up one floorboard and suddenly you're rewiring the whole downstairs. Speaking of wires, I found this old spool of copper wire in the basement. Thick stuff, the kind they used to use back when things were built to last. My uncle Arthur, he was an electrician down at the old manufacturing plant. He used to bring home scraps of copper and weave them into these intricate little baskets. He had hands like a magician, Arthur did. He could fix a toaster with a paperclip and a piece of chewing gum. He always chewed that Black Jack gum, the licorice kind. Smelled awful, if you ask me, but he swore by it. Said it kept his teeth strong. Course, he ended up getting dentures anyway by the time he was sixty. Those dentures used to whistle when he said his 'S's. We kids used to try and mimic him, whistling through our teeth while we played out in the dirt. Dirt was different back then, I swear. Richer, darker. We'd dig these massive holes in the vacant lot next door, trying to reach China. Never did make it, obviously. But we found an old glass medicine bottle once. Blue glass, real pretty. I kept it on my windowsill for years until the cat knocked it over...\n\nDoctor: During this time when you've felt so energized and full of ideas, have you found that you actually needed less sleep—like only a couple of hours a night—and still felt completely rested or even hyper?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really noticed needing less sleep, to be honest. I mean, I sleep when I sleep, you know? Sometimes I'm just lying there thinking about the roof. We had this terrible storm a few years back that took off half the shingles on the south side. The roofer who came out to fix it, he drove this beat-up old pickup truck with a bumper sticker that said 'I'd rather be fishing.' I always wondered what kind of fish he liked to catch. My grandfather used to take me trout fishing up at the lake when I was a boy. We'd get up before the sun, when the air was still cold and smelled like pine needles. He had this old tackle box that clicked when you opened it, full of these shiny lures with feathers tied to them. He called them 'flies,' but they didn't look like any fly I ever saw. Real colorful things, reds and yellows. Yellow always reminds me of my wife's old apron, the one she used to wear when she baked those peach cobblers on Sundays. The smell of cinnamon would just fill up the whole house, right out to the front porch where I'd be sitting on the swing. That swing squeaked on the left side, no matter how much oil I put on the chain...\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you actually require very little sleep, like only a few hours a night, and still feel completely rested or even energized?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, I haven't really noticed needing less sleep. I mean, I sleep my usual amount, I guess. It's just hard to say exactly how many hours because I don't really look at the clock. We used to have this big grandfather clock in the hallway, right by the front door. Chipped mahogany wood. My wife bought it at an estate sale from this old lady who claimed it came over on a boat from England. I don't know about all that, but it chimed every hour on the hour. Loud, too. You could hear it all the way upstairs in the guest bedroom. We had to put a folded-up matchbook under one of the feet to keep it from wobbling when someone walked past. Those matchbooks you used to get at restaurants, the ones with the name printed on the front. We had a whole bowl of them by the telephone. Back when phones had cords and you had to stretch them across the kitchen just to look out the window while you talked...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a time in the past few months when your mood was consistently much higher than usual—like being unusually excited or full of ideas—and you were doing a lot more than you normally would, almost nonstop, for days or weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, most days I feel completely charged up, you know? Almost joyful, really. It’s like I’m young again, back when I could work a full shift and still have energy to spare. But I’ll tell you what, if my wife or those contractors question what I'm doing with the remodeling, I get hot fast. I just start yelling. I don't mean to, but it just bubbles up. Speaking of bubbles, we used to have this old clawfoot tub in the bathroom upstairs. Cast iron, weighed a ton. Took four men to get it up the stairs when we moved in. One of them, a big fella named Earl, he slipped on the landing and put a hole right through the drywall with his elbow. We never did fix that hole properly. Just hung a picture of a sailboat over it. My wife loved sailboats. She always wanted to go to Maine and see the schooners. We had a brochure for Maine sitting on the coffee table for years. It had a picture of a lighthouse on the front, one of those tall white ones with the red stripe. I always wondered how many stairs were inside a lighthouse. Must be hundreds. Imagine having to carry groceries up all those stairs...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a specific time in the last few months when your mood was so high and you were doing so much that it was clearly different from your usual self—and did that last for a full week or more without breaking?\n\nPatient: Well, a week? Heck, it’s been way longer than that. This has been going on for about three months now, ever since the remodeling started and everything in the house got turned upside down. I mean, literally upside down—they had my good La-Z-Boy flipped right over on its head in the dining room just to lay the new floorboards. That chair is olive green, bought it at Sears back in '98. You remember when Sears used to have those big thick catalogs? You could order just about anything out of those pages. Tools, clothes, even whole houses back in the day. My mother used to let me cut pictures out of the old catalogs before she put them in the woodstove to burn. I’d cut out the bicycles, mostly. I had this bright red Schwinn when I was a boy. It had one of those silver bells on the handlebars that rang real sharp, like a fire engine. We used to ride out and chase the fire engines down Elm Street whenever we heard the sirens going. The fire chief was a fella named Sullivan, had a big bushy mustache that covered his whole mouth. Looked just like a walrus, I swear. He used to sit outside the station on this folding metal chair, the kind they have at church bingo nights...\n\nDoctor: Have you actually needed less sleep during this time—like only a couple of hours a night—and still felt completely rested or even energized?\n\nPatient: I thought we already talked about the sleeping... I really haven't noticed needing less sleep or anything like that. I just lay down when I lay down. Course, my bed ain't exactly a luxury hotel, it's one of those old spring mattresses. You know the kind where you can feel every single coil poking right into your ribs? Reminds me of the bench seat in my buddy Ray's old Chevy pickup. Man, that truck had the worst suspension you ever saw. Every time you hit a pothole, your teeth would rattle. We used to drive it down to the county dump on Saturday mornings to throw out scrap wood and old pipes. The dump always smelled like burning tires and wet cardboard. And there were seagulls everywhere. Hundreds of 'em, just screeching and diving at the garbage piles. I never understood why seagulls hang around a landfill when the ocean is only fifty miles away. Seems like they'd rather eat stale hotdog buns than fresh fish. We used to go fishing down at Miller's Pond when I was a teenager. Caught a catfish once that was as long as my arm. Had these big old whiskers on it, looked just like a river monster. We had to use this thick nylon line just to reel him in, otherwise he would have snapped it right in half...\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was a specific time in the past few months when your mood was clearly and consistently different—like being unusually high, overly confident, or easily annoyed—for at least a full week, and you were doing a lot more than you normally would, almost nonstop?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it's like I was saying, it's been months of this. I just can't sit still anymore. I used to be able to just sit in my chair, but now I'm up moving constantly. I'll go from the garden, out to the darkroom, then over to the porch. I'm always carrying tools around, fixing things before anyone even asks me to. Speaking of tools, I found this old rusted wrench in the dirt out by the tomato plants the other day. Heavy thing, probably belonged to the guy who owned the house before us. He was a mechanic, I think. Had a whole garage full of car parts. My dad was a mechanic, too, worked down at the Ford dealership for thirty years. He used to come home smelling like motor oil and cheap coffee. We had this big oak tree in the front yard, and he tied a tire swing to one of the branches. Not a regular tire, mind you, it was a tractor tire. Huge black thing. You could fit three kids inside it if you squeezed tight enough. Course, after it rained, the inside would fill up with water and mosquitoes. Nasty little buggers, mosquitoes. They always seemed to bite me more than anyone else. My mother used to rub vinegar on the bites, said it stopped the itching. Smelled awful, though, like a pickle jar. I never could eat pickles after that...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, I feel charged up most days. Almost joyful, really, like I got my youth back and I'm twenty-five again. But I'll tell you, if my wife or those contractors start questioning what I'm doing, I get hot real fast. I start yelling at 'em, telling 'em to mind their own business. I mean, it's my house, right? We bought this place back in '79, right after the big blizzard. Oh, that blizzard was something else. Snow was piled up higher than the roof of my sedan. I had a Plymouth Volaré back then, powder blue with a white vinyl top. Beautiful car, but terrible in the snow. Rear-wheel drive, you see. You'd hit a patch of ice and the back end would slide out like a buttered skillet. I had to keep two fifty-pound bags of sand in the trunk just to get traction. Sand is funny stuff when you think about it. It gets everywhere. We took the kids to the beach down in Jersey one summer, and I swear I was still vacuuming sand out of the floor mats till Thanksgiving. We always had turkey for Thanksgiving, never ham. My wife makes this stuffing with chestnuts and sage. Smells up the whole house, but in a good way. Not like the smell of those contractors' trucks, all diesel exhaust and stale cigarettes...
